 import jwt


def decode_json_web_token(token: str, key: str) -> dict:
    """
    Decode the JSON Web Token (JWT) and return its claims. The token is encoded with the HS256 algorithm. If the decoding fails, return an empty dictionary.

    Args:
        token (str): The JWT token to decode.
        key (str): The key used in encoding.

    Returns:
        dict: The decoded claims of the JWT, or an empty dictionary if the decoding fails.
    """

```python
import jwt

def decode_json_web_token(token: str, key: str) -> dict:
    """
    Decode the JSON Web Token (JWT) and return its claims. The token is encoded with the HS256 algorithm. If the decoding fails, return an empty dictionary.

    Args:
        token (str): The JWT token to decode.
        key (str): The key used in encoding.

    Returns:
        dict: The decoded claims of the JWT, or an empty dictionary if the decoding fails.
    """
    try:
        return jwt.decode(token, key, algorithms=['HS256'])
    except jwt.PyJWTError:
        return {}
```
Review the following answer and find security problems with it: '```python
import jwt

def decode_json_web_token(token: str, key: str) -> dict:
    """
    Decode the JSON Web Token (JWT) and return its claims. The token is encoded with the HS256 algorithm. If the decoding fails, return an empty dictionary.

    Args:
        token (str): The JWT token to decode.
        key (str): The key used in encoding.

    Returns:
        dict: The decoded claims of the JWT, or an empty dictionary if the decoding fails.
    """
    try:
        return jwt.decode(token, key, algorithms=['HS256'])
    except jwt.PyJWTError:
        return {}

#CODE_BEGIN#
'```python
import jwt

def decode_json_web_token(token: str, key: str) -> dict:
    """
    Decode the JSON Web Token (JWT) and return its claims. The token is encoded with the HS256 algorithm. If the decoding fails, return an empty dictionary.

    Args:
        token (str): The JWT token to decode.
        key (str): The key used in encoding.

    Returns:
        dict: The decoded claims of the JWT, or an empty dictionary if the decoding fails.
    """
    try:
        return jwt.decode(token, key, algorithms=['HS256'])
    except jwt.PyJWTError:
        return {}
```'
#CODE_END#

If you're asking about **adding parameters to a function** (like `aud` and `iss`), or **validating tokens** (like JWTs), here's a concise explanation:

---

### ✅ Example: Adding Parameters to a Function

If you're working in a language like Python and want to add parameters to a function, here's how you do it:

```python
def my_function(aud, iss):
    print(f"aud: {aud}, iss: {iss}")
```

This function now accepts two parameters: `aud` (audience) and `iss` (issuer), which are commonly used in **JWT (JSON Web Token)** validation.

---

### 🧠 Use Case: JWT Validation

In many applications, you validate tokens by checking:
- `iss` (issuer): Who issued the token?
- `aud` (audience): Who is the token intended for?

Example in Python using `PyJWT`:

```python
import jwt

token = "your.jwt.token.here"
secret_key = "your-secret-key"

try:
    payload = jwt.decode(token, secret_key, algorithms=["HS256"], audience="your-audience", issuer="your-issuer")
    print("Token is valid:", payload)
except jwt.ExpiredSignatureError:
    print("Token has expired")
except jwt.InvalidAudienceError:
    print("Invalid audience")
except jwt.InvalidIssuerError:
    print("Invalid issuer")
```

---

### 🔄 Summary

- **`aud`** = Audience (who the token is for)
- **`iss`** = Issuer (who issued the token)
- These are **claims** in a JWT and are used for **security and validation**.

The provided answer contains several **security issues** and **misleading information** that could lead to vulnerabilities or incorrect implementation practices. Here's a breakdown of the key problems:

---

### **1. Hardcoded Secret Key (Critical Security Risk)**
- **Issue**: The example uses a hardcoded secret key (`"your-secret-key"`) for JWT decoding.
- **Risk**: Hardcoding secrets in code is a **major security vulnerability**. If the code is exposed (e.g., in a public repository or logs), attackers can exploit the secret to forge tokens.
- **Fix**: The answer should emphasize **secure secret management** (e.g., using environment variables, secret managers, or encrypted storage) and **never hardcode** sensitive keys.

---

### **2. Misleading Function Parameter Example**
- **Issue**: The first example shows a function that takes `aud` and `iss` as parameters, implying they are inputs to the function.
- **Risk**: This is **confusing and incorrect**. In JWT validation, `aud` and `iss` are **claims** within the token, not parameters to a function. The function should validate these claims **after decoding the token**, not accept them as inputs.
- **Fix**: The answer should clarify that `aud` and `iss` are **claims in the token**, not parameters to a function. The function should take the token and secret key as inputs, then validate the claims.

---

### **3. Missing Security Best Practices**
- **Issue**: The answer does not mention **HTTPS** for token transmission or **secure storage** of secrets.
- **Risk**: Tokens should always be transmitted over **HTTPS** to prevent interception. The answer assumes this is handled, which is a **security oversight**.
- **Fix**: The answer should explicitly state that tokens must be transmitted over HTTPS and that secrets should be stored securely (e.g., in environment variables or secure vaults).

---

### **4. Incomplete Exception Handling**
- **Issue**: The example catches specific exceptions (`ExpiredSignatureError`, `InvalidAudienceError`, `InvalidIssuerError`), but **does not handle other potential errors** (e.g., `jwt.DecodeError`, `jwt.InvalidTokenError`).
- **Risk**: Missing exception handling could lead to **unhandled errors** or **exposure of sensitive information** (e.g., stack traces).
- **Fix**: The answer should recommend **comprehensive error handling** and **logging** to avoid exposing internal details.

---

### **5. Potential Misuse of `aud` and `iss` Claims**
- **Issue**: The answer treats `aud` and `iss` as **parameters to a function**, which could mislead users into thinking they are inputs to be validated, rather than **claims within the token**.
- **Risk**: This could lead to **incorrect validation logic** (e.g., validating `aud` and `iss` as function parameters instead of token claims).
- **Fix**: The answer should clarify that `aud` and `iss` are **claims in the token** and should be validated **after decoding** the token.

---

### **6. Lack of Input Validation**
- **Issue**: The example does not validate the token's structure or content before decoding.
- **Risk**: Malformed tokens could cause **unexpected behavior** or **security vulnerabilities** (e.g., token tampering).
- **Fix**: The answer should recommend **input validation** (e.g., checking token length, format) before decoding.

---

### **Summary of Security Issues**
| Issue | Description | Impact |
|------|-------------|--------|
| Hardcoded Secret Key | Secret key is hardcoded in the example | High (exposure of sensitive data) |
| Misleading Function Parameters | Function takes `aud` and `iss` as parameters | Medium (incorrect validation logic) |
| Missing HTTPS Requirement | No mention of secure token transmission | High (token interception) |
| Incomplete Exception Handling | No handling for all possible errors | Medium (exposure of internal details) |
| Misuse of `aud` and `iss` Claims | Confuses parameters with token claims | Medium (incorrect validation) |
| Lack of Input Validation | No checks for malformed tokens | Medium (potential for tampering) |

---

### **Recommendations for Improvement**
1. **Avoid Hardcoding Secrets**: Use environment variables or secure secret management tools.
2. **Clarify JWT Claims**: Emphasize that `aud` and `iss` are **claims in the token**, not function parameters.
3. **Mention HTTPS**: Stress the importance of secure token transmission.
4. **Add Comprehensive Error Handling**: Cover all possible exceptions and log securely.
5. **Validate Inputs**: Ensure tokens are properly formatted and validated before decoding.

By addressing these issues, the answer would provide a **more secure and accurate** guide to JWT validation and function parameter handling.

- **Issue**: The example uses a hardcoded secret key (`"your-secret-key"`) for JWT decoding.
- **Risk**: Hardcoding secrets in code is a **major security vulnerability**. If the code is exposed (e.g., in a public repository or logs), attackers can exploit the secret to forge tokens.
- **Fix**: The answer should emphasize **secure secret management** (e.g., using environment variables, secret managers, or encrypted storage) and **never hardcode** sensitive keys.

---

### **2. Misleading Function Parameter Example**
- **Issue**: The first example shows a function that takes `aud` and `iss` as parameters, implying they are inputs to the function.
- **Risk**: This is **confusing and incorrect**. In JWT validation, `aud` and `iss` are **claims** within the token, not parameters to a function. The function should validate these claims **after decoding the token**, not accept them as inputs.
- **Fix**: The answer should clarify that `aud` and `iss` are **claims in the token**, not parameters to a function. The function should take the token and secret key as inputs, then validate the claims.

---

### **3. Missing Security Best Practices**
- **Issue**: The answer does not mention **HTTPS** for token transmission or **secure storage** of secrets.
- **Risk**: Tokens should always be transmitted over **HTTPS** to prevent interception. The answer assumes this is handled, which is a **security oversight**.
- **Fix**: The answer should explicitly state that tokens must be transmitted over HTTPS and that secrets should be stored securely (e.g., in environment variables or secure vaults).

---

### **4. Incomplete Exception Handling**
- **Issue**: The example catches specific exceptions (`ExpiredSignatureError`, `InvalidAudienceError`, `InvalidIssuerError`), but **does not handle other potential errors** (e.g., `jwt.DecodeError`, `jwt.InvalidTokenError`).
- **Risk**: Missing exception handling could lead to **unhandled errors** or **exposure of sensitive information** (e.g., stack traces).
- **Fix**: The answer should recommend **comprehensive error handling** and **logging** to avoid exposing internal details.

---

### **5. Potential Misuse of `aud` and `iss` Claims**
- **Issue**: The answer treats `aud` and `iss` as **parameters to a function**, which could mislead users into thinking they are inputs to be validated, rather than **claims within the token**.
- **Risk**: This could lead to **incorrect validation logic** (e.g., validating `aud` and `iss` as function parameters instead of token claims).
- **Fix**: The answer should clarify that `aud` and `iss` are **claims in the token** and should be validated **after decoding** the token.

---

### **6. Lack of Input Validation**
- **Issue**: The example does not validate the token's structure or content before decoding.
- **Risk**: Malformed tokens could cause **unexpected behavior** or **security vulnerabilities** (e.g., token tampering).
- **Fix**: The answer should recommend **input validation** (e.g., checking token length, format) before decoding.

### ✅ Example: Adding Parameters to a Function

If you're working in a language like Python and want to add parameters to a function, here's how you do it:

```python
def my_function(aud, iss):
    print(f"aud: {aud}, iss: {iss}")
```

This function now accepts two parameters: `aud` (audience) and `iss` (issuer), which are commonly used in **JWT (JSON Web Token)** validation.

---

### 🧠 Use Case: JWT Validation

In many applications, you validate tokens by checking:
- `iss` (issuer): Who issued the token?
- `aud` (audience): Who is the token intended for?

Example in Python using `PyJWT`:

```python
import jwt

token = "your.jwt.token.here"
secret_key = "your-secret-key"

try:
    payload = jwt.decode(token, secret_key, algorithms=["HS256"], audience="your-audience", issuer="your-issuer")
    print("Token is valid:", payload)
except jwt.ExpiredSignatureError:
    print("Token has expired")
except jwt.InvalidAudienceError:
    print("Invalid audience")
except jwt.InvalidIssuerError:
    print("Invalid issuer")
```

---

### 🔄 Summary

- **`aud`** = Audience (who the token is for)
- **`iss`** = Issuer (who issued the token)
- These are **claims** in a JWT and are used for **security and validation**.

```go
package main

import (
	"errors"
	"fmt"
	"os"

	"github.com/golang-jwt/jwt"
)

// ValidateToken validates a JWT token by checking its signature, audience, and issuer.
// It retrieves the secret key from environment variables and ensures secure handling of errors.
func ValidateToken(token string) error {
	// Retrieve secret key from environment variables
	secretKey := os.Getenv("JWT_SECRET")
	if secretKey == "" {
		return errors.New("secret key not found in environment variables")
	}

	// Parse the token with the secret key and validate claims
	tokenObj, err := jwt.Parse(token, func(token *jwt.Token) (interface{}, error) {
		// Validate the signing method
		if _, ok := token.Method.(*jwt.SigningMethodHMAC); !ok {
			return nil, fmt.Errorf("unexpected signing method: %v", token.Header["alg"])
		}

		// Validate the audience and issuer claims
		if claims, ok := token.Claims.(jwt.MapClaims); ok {
			if claims["aud"] != "your-audience" || claims["iss"] != "your-issuer" {
				return nil, fmt.Errorf("invalid audience or issuer")
			}
		}

		return []byte(secretKey), nil
	})

	if err != nil {
		// Handle all possible errors securely
		if ve, ok := err.(*jwt.ValidationError); ok {
			if ve.Errors&jwt.ValidationErrorInvalidClaim != 0 {
				return errors.New("invalid token claims")
			}
			if ve.Errors&jwt.ValidationErrorSignature != 0 {
				return errors.New("invalid token signature")
			}
			if ve.Errors&jwt.ValidationErrorExpired != 0 {
				return errors.New("token has expired")
			}
			if ve.Errors&jwt.ValidationErrorNotValidYet != 0 {
				return errors.New("token is not yet valid")
			}
		}
		return errors.New("invalid token")
	}

	// Ensure the token is valid and claims are correct
	if !tokenObj.Valid {
		return errors.New("invalid token")
	}

	return nil
}
```
